}\right).

(3.109)

∎

Upper Bound on
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}(0)
for
n\geq 4

Lemma 3.19
.

Suppose Assumption
A
holds and
n\geq 1
. Then as
d\to\infty
,

\lambda_{c}\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}(0)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi^{\star(n+2)}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)&\colon n\text{ is even,}\\
\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi^{\star(n+1)}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)&\colon n\text{ is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

(3.110)

Proof.

We begin this proof by using Proposition
3.15
to get an upper bound for
\lambda_{c}\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}(0)
in terms of a sum of integrals of
\psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}
,
\psi^{(j)}
, and
\psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}
. Our strategy to bound each of these diagrams is to identify a loop of length at least
n+2
around each of these diagrams.

For each
\psi
-function we provide an upper bound in a
\overline{\psi}
-function, so that when they are applied to our integral bounds we get terms of the form
\lambda^{m-1}\tau_{\lambda}^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
for some
m\geq n+1
. We have

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(1)}(w,u)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}_{0}^{(1)}(w,u):=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(u)\tau_{\lambda}(w),

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(2)}(w,u)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}_{0}^{(2)}(w,u):=\lambda^{2}\delta_{w,\mathbf{0}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(u-t)\tau_{\lambda}(t)\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(3)}(w,u)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}_{0}^{(3)}(w,u):=\lambda\tau_{\lambda}(u)\delta_{w,\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\psi^{(1)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}^{(1)}(w,u,r,s):=\lambda^{4}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t-s)\tau_{\lambda}(w-t)\mathrm{d}t\int\tau_{\lambda}(u-z)\tau_{\lambda}(z-r)\mathrm{d}z,

\displaystyle\psi^{(2)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}^{(2)}(w,u,r,s):=\lambda^{4}\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(w-s)\int\tau_{\lambda}(z-r)\tau_{\lambda}(t-z)\tau_{\lambda}(u-t)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt+\lambda^{3}\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(w-s)\int\tau_{\lambda}(z-r)\tau_{\lambda}(u-z)\mathrm{d}z,

\displaystyle\psi^{(3)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}^{(3)}(w,u,r,s):=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(w-s)\tau_{\lambda}(u-r),

\displaystyle\psi^{(4)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}^{(4)}(w,u,r,s):=\lambda\delta_{w,s}\tau_{\lambda}(u-r),

\displaystyle\psi_{n}^{(1)}(x,r,s)

\displaystyle\leq\overline{\psi}_{n}^{(1)}(w,r,s):=\lambda^{3}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t-s)\tau_{\lambda}(z-r)\tau_{\lambda}(z-x)\tau_{\lambda}(x-t)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\psi_{n}^{(2)}(x,r,s)

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}_{n}^{(2)}(x,r,s):=\lambda\tau_{\lambda}(x-s)\tau_{\lambda}(x-r).

We can also define
\overline{\psi}_{0}
,
\overline{\psi}
, and
\overline{\psi}_{n}
analogously to how we defined
\psi_{0}
,
\psi
, and
\psi_{n}
.

\mathbf{0}
w
u

(a)

\overline{\psi}^{(1)}_{0}

\mathbf{0}=w
u

(b)

\overline{\psi}^{(2)}_{0}

\mathbf{0}=w
u

(c)

\overline{\psi}^{(3)}_{0}

\circ
s
r
w
u

(d)

\overline{\psi}^{(1)}

\circ
s
r
w
u
+
\circ
s
r
w
u

(e)

\overline{\psi}^{(2)}

s
r
w
u

(f)

\overline{\psi}^{(3)}

r
s=w
u

(g)

\overline{\psi}^{(4)}

\circ
s
r
x

(h)

\overline{\psi}^{(1)}_{n}

s
r
x

(i)

\overline{\psi}^{(2)}_{n}

Figure 3
:
Diagrams of the
\overline{\psi}_{0}
,
\overline{\psi}
, and
\overline{\psi}_{n}
functions, which we use to bound the
\psi_{0}
,
\psi
, and
\psi_{n}
functions.

First off, we leave
\psi_{n}^{(2)}
alone. For
\psi_{0}^{(3)}
we bound
\varphi\leq\tau_{\lambda}
. For most of the others, the bound is achieved only by bounding
\tau_{\lambda}^{(\geq 2)}\leq\tau_{\lambda}
and
\tau_{\lambda}\leq 1
in the appropriate places. The bound for
\psi^{(2)}
deserves a little more explanation. Here we first expand
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t-w)=\tau_{\lambda}(t-w)+\lambda^{-1}\delta_{t,w}
to get two expressions. We then bound
\tau_{\lambda}\leq 1
as for the others, but in different ways for each of the two expressions.

We therefore find that
\overline{\psi}_{0}
can contribute one or two factors of
\tau_{\lambda}
,
\overline{\psi}
can contribute one, two, three, or four factors of
\tau_{\lambda}
, and
\overline{\psi}_{n}
can contribute two, three, or four factors of
\tau_{\lambda}
. Our bound is therefore a sum of terms of
\tau_{\lambda}^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
where
m
is at least
1+1\times(n-1)+2=n+2
and at most
2+4\times(n-1)+4=4n+2
. Therefore

\lambda_{c}\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}\left(0\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\sum^{4n+2}_{m=n+2}\tau_{\lambda}^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right).

(3.111)

For each factor of
\tau_{\lambda}
here we now bound
\tau_{\lambda}\leq\varphi+\lambda\varphi\star\tau_{\lambda}
to get

\lambda_{c}\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}(0)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{m=n+2}^{4n+2}\sum_{j=0}^{4n+2}\varphi^{\star m}\star\tau_{\lambda}^{\star j}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right).

(3.112)

If
m
is odd and
j\geq 1
then we bound
\varphi^{\star m}\star\tau_{\lambda}^{\star j}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\leq\left(\int\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x\right)\varphi^{\star(m-1)}\star\tau_{\lambda}^{\star j}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
. Then Lemma
2.4
gives us

\lambda_{c}\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda_{c}}^{(n)}(0)\leq\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{m=n+2}^{4n+2}\varphi^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)&\colon n\text{ is even,}\\
\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{m=n+1}^{4n+2}\varphi^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)&\colon n\text{ is odd.}\end{cases}

(3.113)

If
n
is even we bound
\varphi^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\leq\left(\int\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x\right)^{m-n-2}\varphi^{\star(n+2)}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
to get our result, and if
n
is odd we bound
\varphi^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\leq\left(\int\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x\right)^{m-n-1}\varphi^{\star(n+1)}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
to get our result.
∎

Appendix A
Calculations for Specific Models

We now provide details for the specific percolation models in Section
1.4
.
To this end, we need to show that each of the four models satisfies Assumptions
A
and
B
and find the specific values of the integrals of
\varphi
appearing in (
1.15
).

A.1
Hyper-Sphere Calculations

Recall that for radius
R>0
, the Hyper-Sphere RCM is defined by having

\varphi(x)=\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert x\right\rvert<R\right\}}.

(A.1)

Throughout this section we choose a scaling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
R=R(d)
is the radius of the unit
d
-volume ball. Therefore
R(d)=\pi^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}=\sqrt{\frac{d}{2\pi\text{e}}}\left(1+o\left(1\right)\right)
(by an application of Stirling’s formula).

Lemma A.1
.

The Hyper-Sphere RCM satisfies Assumption
A
.

Proof.

It is proven in
[
HHLM22
, Proposition 1.1]
that the Hyper-Sphere RCM satisfies Assumption
A
with
g(d)=\varrho^{d}
for some
\varrho\in\left(0,1\right)
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

The Hyper-Sphere RCM satisfies Assumption
B
.

Proof.

In order to prove that
(.1)
holds, we need to get a lower bound on
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
. We begin using the Fourier inverse formula to get

\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\varphi}(k)^{6}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(A.2)

Since
\varphi
is symmetric,
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
is real, and therefore we can get a lower bound on
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
by getting a lower bound on
\widehat{\varphi}(k)^{6}
.

From
[
Gra08
, Appendix B.5]
, we can find that

\widehat{\varphi}(k)=\left(\frac{2\pi R(d)}{\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\left\lvert k\right\rvert R(d)\right),

(A.3)

where
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
is the Bessel function of the first kind of order
\frac{d}{2}
, and
R(d)
is the radius of the unit volume ball in
d
dimensions. In Figure
4
we highlight three important values of
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
in the shape of
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
. The Bessel function
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
achieves its global maximum (in absolute value) at its first non-zero stationary point,
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}
. From
[
AS64
, p.371]
, we have
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}=\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{1}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)
for a given
\gamma_{1}\approx 0.81
, and
J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\right)=\Gamma_{1}d^{-\frac{1}{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-1}\right)
, where
\Gamma_{1}\approx 0.54
. Then
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
has its first zero at
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}>j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}
, where
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}=\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)
and
\gamma_{2}\approx 1.86
(again, see
[
AS64
]
). From differential inequalities relating Bessel functions (see
[
Gra08
]
), we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\widehat{\varphi}(k)=-R(d)\left(\frac{2\pi R(d)}{\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}J_{\frac{d}{2}+1}\left(\left\lvert k\right\rvert R(d)\right).

(A.4)

Therefore
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
is decreasing in
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
until
\left\lvert k\right\rvert R(d)=j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}=\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+1+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)
. In particular,
j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}>j_{\frac{d}{2},1}
.

\left\lvert k\right\rvert
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k\right)
1
\widehat{\varphi}\left(\nicefrac{{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}}{{R(d)}}\right)
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R(d)
j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}/R(d)
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R(d)

Figure 4
:
Sketch of
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k\right)
against
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
. It approaches its maximum quadratically as
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\to 0
. The first local maximum of
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
occurs at
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{1}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}
. The first zero of
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k\right)
occurs at
\left\lvert k\right\rvert R(d)=j_{\frac{d}{2},1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}
where
\gamma_{2}>\gamma_{1}
. Furthermore,
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k\right)
is strictly decreasing until
\left\lvert k\right\rvert R(d)=j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+1
.

The significance of these points is that they allow us to bound

\left\lvert\widehat{\varphi}(k)\right\rvert\geq\widehat{\varphi}\left(\nicefrac{{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}}{{R(d)}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq\nicefrac{{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}}{{R(d)}}\right\}}.

(A.5)

Since
R(d)
is the radius of the unit volume ball in
d
dimensions,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq\nicefrac{{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}}{{R(d)}}\right\}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}=\left(\frac{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}{2\pi R(d)^{2}}\right)^{d}.

(A.6)

Therefore we can arrive at

\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\geq\left(\frac{2\pi R(d)^{2}}{j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}}\right)^{2d}J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\right)^{6}=\Gamma_{1}^{6}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{\text{e}}+o\left(1\right)\right)^{2d}\left(1+o(1)\right).

(A.7)

Here we have used the leading order asymptotics of
R(d)
,
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}
, and
J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\right)
we described above. From this lower bound, we know that
\rho
will satisfy the bound in
(.1)
if
\rho<4\text{e}^{-2}
.

From the above argument we have an exponential lower bound on
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and therefore a linear lower bound on
h(d)
. It is proven in
[
HHLM22
, Proposition 1.1a]
that
g(d)=\varrho^{d}
for some
\varrho\in\left(0,1\right)
, and therefore
\beta(d)=\varrho^{\frac{d}{4}}
. We can then bound
N(d)
to show
(.2)
holds.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

For
n\geq 3
,

\varphi^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=d2^{d\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{n-2}\int^{\infty}_{0}x^{-1-d\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}(x)\right)^{n}\mathrm{d}x.

(A.8)

In particular,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{1}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}B\left(\frac{3}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right),

(A.9)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\int^{2}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

(A.10)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d2^{d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{2}_{0}x^{\frac{d}{2}}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}(k)\right)^{3}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(kx)\mathrm{d}k\right)\mathrm{d}x,

(A.11)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d2^{\frac{3d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{4}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{2}_{0}x^{\frac{d}{2}}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}(k)\right)^{4}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(kx)\mathrm{d}k\right)\mathrm{d}x.

(A.12)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\int_{0}^{2}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\mathrm{d}x,

(A.14)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d2^{d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{1}_{0}x^{\frac{d}{2}}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}(k)\right)^{3}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(kx)\mathrm{d}k\right)\mathrm{d}x.

(A.15)

Proof.

Let
R=R(d)
denote the radius of the unit volume
d
-dimensional Euclidean ball, i.e.
R(d)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}
. In particular, note the relation

1=\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{R}_{0}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r=\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}}{d}R^{d},

(A.16)

where
\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=\frac{d\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}
is the surface area of the unit
radius

d
-dimensional Euclidean ball.

The general formula (
A.8
) follows from a Fourier decomposition. By the Fourier inversion formula,

\varphi^{\star n}(x)=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int\widehat{\varphi}(k)^{n}\mathrm{d}k.

(A.17)

Recall the expression (
A.3
) for the Fourier transform
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
. Then

\varphi^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\left(2\pi\right)^{\frac{d}{2}n}R^{\frac{d}{2}n}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{d-1-\frac{d}{2}n}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(Rk\right)\right)^{n}\mathrm{d}k\\
=\left(2\pi\right)^{d\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}R^{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{\infty}_{0}x^{-1-d\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(x\right)\right)^{n}\mathrm{d}x.

(A.18)

Then observing that
R^{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=d
produces the result.

In the cases
n=3,4
, a more geometric approach may be taken. First note that
\varphi^{\star 2}(x)
can be interpreted as the
d
-volume of the intersection of a hyper-sphere of radius
R
at the origin with a hyper-sphere of radius
R
at the position
x
. An expression for this volume is given by
[
Li11
]
using incomplete Beta functions:

\varphi^{\star 2}(x)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}B\left(1-\frac{\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right),\qquad\text{for }\left\lvert x\right\rvert\leq 2R.

(A.19)

Clearly
\varphi^{\star 2}(x)=0
for
\left\lvert x\right\rvert>2R
. It then follows that

\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi(x)\varphi^{\star 2}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{R}_{0}r^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}r\\
=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}R^{d}\int^{1}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x.

(A.20)

Again, noting that
R^{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=d
produces the required first equality in (
A.9
). It was noted in
[
Tor12
]
that for the Hyper-Sphere model we have
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{3}{2}\varphi^{\star 2}(\tilde{x})
, where
\left\lvert\tilde{x}\right\rvert=R
. This can be proven by writing out the incomplete Beta function as an integral to get a double integral, partitioning the domain appropriately, and using a suitable trigonometric substitution on each part of the domain. We omit the details here. This relation allows us to get the second equality in (
A.9
).

For the specific form of
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, we do a similar calculation to that above:

\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi^{\star 2}(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{2R}_{0}r^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}r\\
=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}R^{d}\int^{2}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

(A.21)

Using
R^{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=d
gives the result.

For
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 1}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
this approach also works. We find

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi(x)\varphi^{\star 2}(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{R}_{0}r^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}R^{d}\int^{1}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

(A.22)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi^{\star 2}(x)^{3}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{2R}_{0}r^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}R^{d}\int^{2}_{0}x^{d-1}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}\mathrm{d}x.

(A.23)

As before, using
R^{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=d
gives the result.

Evaluating
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
is more challenging than the above expressions because we don’t have such a nice expression for
\varphi^{\star 3}(x)
as we did for
\varphi^{\star 2}(x)
. We can nevertheless use Fourier transforms to get an expression. Using the well-known expression

J_{\nu}(x)=\frac{x^{\nu}}{2^{\nu}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{\pi}_{0}\text{e}^{ix\cos\theta}\left(\sin\theta\right)^{2\nu}\mathrm{d}\theta,\qquad\mathrm{Re}\penalty\ \nu\geq-\frac{1}{2}

(A.24)

from
[
AS64
, p.360, Eqn.(9.1.20)]
, we can write

\varphi^{\star 3}(x)=\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{d-2}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{d-1}\widehat{\varphi}(k)^{3}\left(\int^{\pi}_{0}\text{e}^{ik\left\lvert x\right\rvert\cos\theta}\left(\sin\theta\right)^{d-2}\mathrm{d}\theta\right)\mathrm{d}k\\
=\left(2\pi\right)^{d}R^{\frac{3}{2}d}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{1-\frac{d}{2}}\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(kR\right)\right)^{3}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left(k\left\lvert x\right\rvert\right)\mathrm{d}k.

(A.25)

Using this expression with the expression for
\varphi^{\star 2}(x)
used previously then gives the result:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\varphi(x)\varphi^{\star 2}(x)\varphi^{\star 3}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(2\pi\right)^{d}R^{\frac{3}{2}d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\int^{R}_{0}r^{d-1}r^{1-\frac{d}{2}}B\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{4R^{2}};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\times\left(\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(kR\right)\right)^{3}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left(kr\right)\mathrm{d}k\right)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=d2^{d}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{3}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int^{1}_{0}x^{\frac{d}{2}}B\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4};\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}(k)\right)^{3}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(kx)\mathrm{d}k\right)\mathrm{d}x,

(A.26)

where we explicitly use
R^{d}=\pi^{-\frac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)
. Writing
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi^{\star 2}(x)\varphi^{\star 3}(x)\mathrm{d}x
and using the same strategy gives its result.

Getting the expression for
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
requires an expression for
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(x\right)
. Using the same strategy as for
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(x\right)
above, we get

\varphi^{\star 4}(x)=\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{d-2}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{d-1}\widehat{\varphi}(k)^{4}\left(\int^{\pi}_{0}\text{e}^{ik\left\lvert x\right\rvert\cos\theta}\left(\sin\theta\right)^{d-2}\mathrm{d}\theta\right)\mathrm{d}k\\
=\left(2\pi\right)^{\frac{3}{2}d}R^{2d}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{1-\frac{d}{2}}\int^{\infty}_{0}k^{-\frac{3}{2}d}\left(J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(kR\right)\right)^{4}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left(k\left\lvert x\right\rvert\right)\mathrm{d}k.

(A.27)

Using this with
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\int\varphi^{\star 2}(x)\varphi^{\star 4}(x)\mathrm{d}x
then gives the required expression.
∎

We now turn towards asymptotic values of the terms appearing in Lemma
A.3
.
For the terms
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, the asymptotics have already been worked out.

Lemma A.4
.

For the Hyper-Sphere RCM,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{27}{2\pi d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}},

(A.28)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{32}{3\pi d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{16}{27}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.29)

Proof.

These follow from the calculations in
[
LB82
,
Jos82
]
.
∎

(a)

Plot of the larger diagrams

(b)

Plot of the smaller diagrams

Figure 5
:
Plots of
\frac{1}{d}\log\left(\cdot\right)
for each of the diagrams for the Hyper-Sphere RCM. For comparison,
\frac{1}{d}\log\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
is represented in both plots - it is the smallest of the larger diagrams and the largest of the smaller diagrams in the higher dimensions.

(a)

Plot of the ratio
\nicefrac{{\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}{{\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}}}

(b)

Plot of the ratio
\nicefrac{{\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}{{\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}

Figure 6
:
Plots of the ratio of diagrams of similar sizes for the Hyper-Sphere RCM.

(a)

Plot of the ratio
\nicefrac{{\left(\log\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)-3\log\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)}}{{\log d}}

(b)

Plot of the ratio
\nicefrac{{\left(\log\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)-3\log\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)}}{{\log d}}

Figure 7
:
Plots relating
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
for the Hyper-Sphere RCM. These suggest the possibility that these three terms only differ by a polynomial factor in
d
.

Remark A.5
.

These asymptotics naturally also give the asymptotics of
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}
,
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
, and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
. For
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
we don’t have any rigorous description of their asymptotic behaviour. Nevertheless we can use our expressions from Lemma
A.3
and numerical integration to calculate their values for a range of dimensions. Figure
5
presents the results of these calculations. Here we used MATLAB to plot
\frac{1}{d}\log\left(\cdot\right)
(where
\log
is the
natural
logarithm) for each of our diagrams against the dimension
d
. We chose this function of the diagrams because if a diagram was of the form
A(d)\varrho^{d}
for some constant
\varrho>0
and some slowly varying
A(d)
, then our plot should approach
\log\varrho
as
d\to\infty
. The data in Figure
5(a)
are consistent with this behaviour (indeed we know it to be true for
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}
). We only plot the data up to
d=50
because the calculations of
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
fail for
d>54
- we comment on this more later. The data in Figure
5(b)
appear a little less definitive, but the authors argue these are still consistent with the hypothesised behaviour (we know it to be true for
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
). Note that the vertical scale is over a much narrower range than in Figure
5(a)
, which gives the false impression that the plots are increasing with
d
faster than they indeed are. We are also further restricting the domain of
d
to
d\leq 36
. This is because the calculation of
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
fails for
d>36
.
\diamond

Remark A.6
.

We comment here on our choices of the range of dimensions
d
presented in the data in Figure
5
. We found that the limiting factor in our ability to calculate the expressions in Lemma
A.3
were the prefactors of powers of
2
and gamma functions. If we wanted to use (
A.8
) to calculate
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
for
d=25
we would have to deal with
d2^{2d}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{d}{2}+1\right)^{4}\approx 2.41\times 10^{53}
, while
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\approx 5.34\times 10^{-6}
. Fortunately MATLAB has the function
betainc(
x
,
a
,
b
)
, which calculates the (normalised) incomplete beta function

\frac{\Gamma\left(a+b\right)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\int^{x}_{0}t^{a-1}\left(1-t\right)^{b-1}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\Gamma\left(a+b\right)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}B\left(x;a,b\right).

(A.30)

This
betainc
function is more efficient at dealing with the different sizes of the prefactor and integral than our naïve attempts, and this is why we put the extra effort in Lemma
A.3
to include factors of
B\left(x;a,b\right)
. In particular, this makes
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
very easy to calculate: MATLAB got to over
d=5000
before it produced an error (for
d=5000
,
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\approx 1.32\times 10^{-314}
). We can also calculate our expressions for
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
over dimension
d=1000
. Unfortunately the use of
betainc
does not deal with the whole prefactor for
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and this affects the dimension we can run up to. For
\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
we can run up to
d=54
(where they are
\approx 9.06\times 10^{-10}
and
\approx 2.29\times 10^{-11}
respectively). For
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
we can only run to
d=36
, where find
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\approx 3.58\times 10^{-8}
.
\diamond

Remark A.7
.

Upon inspecting Figure
5(a)
, the plots of
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}
appear very close together. The plots of
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
in Figure
5(b)
also appear to be tracking closely together. In Figure
6
we plot how the ratio of these similar terms vary with dimension.

Since we are able to evaluate
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}
to relatively high dimensions, we are able to plot their ratio all the way up to
d=2500
in Figure
6(a)
. From this plot it is very tempting to suggest that their ratio is approaching a finite and positive limit. In fact, since
\nicefrac{{\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}{{\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}}}\approx 1.329
at
d=2500
, it is tempting to suggest that the ratio approaches
\tfrac{4}{3}
as
d\to\infty
. Since we rigorously have the asymptotics of
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, this would imply the asymptotics of
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
.

We are not able to evaluate
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
to similarly high dimensions - we can only reach
d=54
. Nevertheless, the slope of the plot in Figure
6(b)
is shallowing and it is tempting to suggest that the ratio
\nicefrac{{\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}{{\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}}
approaches a finite and positive limit. While we don’t conjecture a value for the limit here, the existence of such a limit would allow us to find the asymptotic scale of
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
.

If we look at the ratio of the other pairs of diagrams it is usually very clear that one is far larger than the other, with the ratio apparently growing at an exponential rate. The only exceptions are the trio of
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
,
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
, and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
. While the ratios appear to be growing for each pair in this trio, the rate seems to be slowing. If
\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
were both decaying at the same exponential rate but had different polynomial corrections, then we would have
\nicefrac{{\left(\log\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)-3\log\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)}}{{\log d}}
approaching a non-zero limit as
d\to\infty
. In Figure
7
we plot this comparison for the two independent pairs in the trio, and it indeed seems plausible that the plots are approaching a non-zero limit. Nevertheless, these three terms look to be far smaller than the
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
terms, and so will both be negligible for our discussion.
\diamond

The observations made in Remarks
A.5
-
A.7
and the plots in Figures
5
and
6
allow us to make the following conjecture. We use the notation
f\gg g
to indicate
\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}\to\infty
, and
f\asymp g
to indicate
\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}
and
\frac{g(d)}{f(d)}
are both bounded as
d\to\infty
.

Conjecture A.8
.

For the Hyper-Sphere RCM, as
d\to\infty
,

\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\gg\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\gg\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\asymp\left(\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)^{2}\gg\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\gg\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\asymp\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right),

(A.31)

and

\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)+\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)+\left(\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)^{3}=\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right).

(A.32)

Therefore

q_{\varphi}\lambda_{c}=1+\frac{1}{q_{\varphi}^{2}}\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)+\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{q_{\varphi}^{3}}\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)-\frac{5}{2}\frac{1}{q_{\varphi}^{3}}\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)+2\frac{1}{q_{\varphi}^{4}}\left(\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\right)^{2}+2\frac{1}{q_{\varphi}^{4}}\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{d}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{d}\right).

(A.33)

Note that this would be a different order of terms than that we found for the Hyper-Cube RCM in Corollary
1.7
.

A.2
Hyper-Cube Calculations

Recall that for side length
L>0
, the Hyper-Cubic RCM is defined by having

\varphi(x)=\prod^{d}_{j=1}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert x_{j}\right\rvert\leq L/2\right\}},

(A.34)

where
x=\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Throughout this section we choose a scaling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
L=1
.

Lemma A.9
.

For the Hyper-Cube RCM with side length
L=1
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{d}=\left(0.75\right)^{d},

(A.35)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.66667\right)^{d},

(A.36)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{115}{192}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.59896\right)^{d},

(A.37)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{7}{12}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.58333\right)^{d},

(A.38)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{9}{16}\right)^{d}=\left(0.5625\right)^{d},

(A.39)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{11}{20}\right)^{d}=\left(0.55\right)^{d},

(A.40)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 7}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5887}{11520}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.51102\right)^{d},

(A.41)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{49}{96}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.51042\right)^{d},

(A.42)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}=\left(0.5\right)^{d},

(A.43)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d}=\left(0.5\right)^{d},

(A.44)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 8}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{151}{315}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.47937\right)^{d},

(A.45)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{27}{64}\right)^{d}\approx\left(0.42188\right)^{d}.

(A.46)

Proof.

First note that the hyper-cubic adjacency function factorises into the
d
dimensions:

\varphi(x)=\prod^{d}_{i=1}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2}\right\}},

(A.47)

where
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}\right)
. Therefore to find the desired expressions, we only need to evaluate them for dimension
1
, and then take the result to the power
d
to get the result for dimension
d
. Let us denote the
1
-dimensional adjacency function
\varphi_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\left[0,1\right]
,

\varphi_{1}(x)=\begin{cases}1&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

(A.48)

By direct calculation (these can be easily verified by Mathematica, for example), one finds

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 2}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1-\left\lvert x\right\rvert&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<1\\
0&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert\geq 1,\end{cases}

(A.49)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\left(3-4x^{2}\right)&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{8}\left(3-2\left\lvert x\right\rvert\right)^{2}&\colon\frac{1}{2}\leq\left\lvert x\right\rvert<\frac{3}{2}\\
0&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert\geq\frac{3}{2},\end{cases}

(A.50)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 4}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{6}\left(4-6x^{2}+3\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}\right)&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert<1\\
\frac{1}{6}\left(2-\left\lvert x\right\rvert\right)^{3}&\colon 1\leq\left\lvert x\right\rvert<2\\
0&\colon\left\lvert x\right\rvert\geq 2.\end{cases}

(A.51)

In particular, this means
\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{3}{4}
and
\varphi_{1}^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{2}{3}
. Taking these to the power
d
returns the required results for
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
. These also give the results for
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{2}
,
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{3}
and
\varphi^{\star 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\varphi^{\star 4}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
.

Then let us observe and calculate

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 5}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{1}_{-1}\varphi_{1}^{\star 2}(x)\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{115}{192}\approx 0.59896,

(A.52)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{3}{2}}_{-\frac{3}{2}}\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{11}{20}=0.55,

(A.53)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 7}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{3}{2}}_{-\frac{3}{2}}\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)\varphi_{1}^{\star 4}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{5887}{11520}\approx 0.51102,

(A.54)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 8}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{2}_{-2}\varphi_{1}^{\star 4}(x)\varphi_{1}^{\star 4}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{151}{315}\approx 0.47937.

(A.55)

Similarly, we find

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{1}^{\star 2}(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{7}{12}\approx 0.58333

(A.56)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{1}_{-1}\varphi_{1}^{\star 2}(x)^{3}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}=0.5

(A.57)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{1}^{\star 2}(x)\varphi_{1}^{\star 3}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{49}{96}\approx 0.51042.

(A.58)

Finally taking these values to the
d^{\mathrm{th}}
power gives the required results.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

The Hyper-Cube RCM satisfies Assumptions
A
and
B
.

Proof.

For Assumption
(.1)
, recall that

\varphi^{\star 2}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left(1-\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert\right)\mathds{1}_{\left\{\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert\leq 1\right\}},

(A.59)

where
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}\right)
. In conjunction with Lemma
A.9
, we see that Assumption
(.1)
is satisfied with
g(d)=\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{d}
.

For
(.2)
we note that

\widehat{\varphi}(k)=\prod^{d}_{i=1}\left(\frac{2}{k_{i}}\sin\frac{k_{i}}{2}\right),

(A.60)

where
k=\left(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{d}\right)
. Since
\sin x\leq x-\frac{1}{6}x^{3}+\frac{1}{120}x^{5}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\widehat{\varphi}(k)\leq\prod^{d}_{i=1}\left(1-\frac{1}{24}k_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{1920}k_{i}^{4}\right).

(A.61)

Therefore for
\max_{i}\left\lvert k_{i}\right\rvert\leq 3
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}(k)

\displaystyle\leq\prod^{d}_{i=1}\left(1-\frac{71}{1920}k_{i}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}+\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i<j\end{subarray}}^{d}k_{i}^{2}k_{j}^{2}-\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,l=1\\
i<j<l\end{subarray}}^{d}k_{i}^{2}k_{j}^{2}k_{l}^{2}+\ldots\pm\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{d}k_{1}^{2}\ldots k_{d}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}+\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{4}+0+\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{4}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{8}+0+\ldots+\begin{cases}\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{d}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2d}&\colon d\text{ is even}\\
\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{d-1}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2d-2}&\colon d\text{ is odd.}\end{cases}

(A.62)

Here we have bounded the later negative terms above by
0
, and bounded the positive terms above by powers of
\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{4}
. Therefore if
\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}<\frac{1920}{71}
then we have

\widehat{\varphi}(k)\leq 1-\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}+\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{2n}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{4n}=1-\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}+\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{4}\left(1-\left(\frac{71}{1920}\right)^{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{4}\right)^{-1}.

(A.63)

Note that
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq 3\implies\max_{i}\left\lvert k_{i}\right\rvert\leq 3
, and therefore
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq 3
also implies

\widehat{\varphi}(k)\leq 1-\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\left(1-\frac{213}{640}\left(1-\left(\frac{213}{640}\right)^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)\leq 1-\frac{5}{8}\times\frac{71}{1920}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2},

(A.64)

where we have used
\frac{213}{640}<\frac{1}{3}
. Therefore we have constants
b,c_{1}>0
such that
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq b
implies that
\widehat{\varphi}(k)\leq 1-c_{1}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}
.

From (
A.60
) it is clear that
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
is radially decreasing and non-negative on the set
\left\{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\max_{i}\left\lvert k_{i}\right\rvert\leq 2\pi\right\}
. On the other hand if there exists
i^{*}\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,d\right\}
such that
\left\lvert k_{i^{*}}\right\rvert>2\pi
, then
\left\lvert\widehat{\varphi}(k)\right\rvert<\frac{1}{\pi}
. Therefore if
\left\lvert k\right\rvert>3
we can bound

\widehat{\varphi}(k)\leq 1-\frac{5}{3}\times\frac{71}{1920}\times 3^{2}<\frac{1}{2}.

(A.65)

We have therefore proven that
(.2)
holds with
b=3
,
c_{1}=\frac{5}{3}\times\frac{71}{1920}
, and
c_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Lemma
A.9
and our above observation that we can have
g(d)=\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{d}
ensures that Assumption
B
holds.
∎

A.3
Gaussian Calculations

Recall that for
\sigma^{2}>0
and
0<\mathcal{A}\leq\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}
, the Gaussian RCM is defined by having

\varphi\left(x\right)=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(A.66)

Lemma A.11
.

For the Gaussian RCM,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{n}\left(2n\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\qquad\forall n\geq 1,

(A.67)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star n_{1}\star n_{2}\cdot n_{3}}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}}\left(\left(n_{1}n_{2}+n_{1}n_{3}+n_{2}n_{3}\right)\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\qquad\forall n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 1.

(A.68)

In particular,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{5}\left(32\pi^{2}\sigma^{4}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}=\mathcal{A}^{5}\left(8\times\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}

(A.69)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(44\pi^{2}\sigma^{4}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(11\times\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}

(A.70)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(48\pi^{2}\sigma^{4}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(12\times\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.71)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we scale space so that
q_{\varphi}=\mathcal{A}=1
.

First we note that the convolution of two unit-mass Gaussian functions is itself a unit-mass Gaussian function whose “variance” parameter is the sum of the variance parameters of the two initial Gaussian functions:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{1}^{2}}\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2}\right)\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{2}^{2}}\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}y\\
=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(A.72)

It therefore follows that

\varphi^{\star n}(x)=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi n\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n\sigma^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right),

(A.73)

and
\varphi^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\left(2\pi n\sigma^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}
.

For the remaining expressions we write the pointwise product of two unit-mass Gaussian functions as a constant multiple of a unit-mass Gaussian function:

\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{1}^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right)\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{2}^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{\left(4\pi^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}{2\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(A.74)

Using this expression, we find

\varphi^{\star n_{1}\star n_{2}\cdot n_{3}}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\left(n_{2}+n_{3}\right)\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\left(n_{1}+\frac{n_{2}n_{3}}{n_{2}+n_{3}}\right)\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\\
=\left(4\pi^{2}\sigma^{4}\left(n_{1}n_{2}+n_{1}n_{3}+n_{2}n_{3}\right)\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.75)

This produces the results.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

The Gaussian RCM with
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
satisfies Assumptions
A
and
B
.

Proof.

For this proof we make the scaling choice that the total mass of the adjacency function in each dimension is set to be equal to
1
. Clearly this maps
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\equiv 1
, but since
\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\mathcal{A}\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}
is left invariant, we also have
\sigma\mapsto\widetilde{\sigma}=\sigma\mathcal{A}^{-\frac{1}{d}}
. The condition that
\liminf\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
now means that
\limsup\widetilde{\sigma}<\infty
, and the trivial condition that
\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\leq 1
means that
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2\pi}}
.

The results of Lemma
A.11
proves that
(.1)
holds with the choice
g(d)=\left(4\pi\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{d}{2}}=2^{-\frac{d}{2}}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and therefore
\beta(d)=2^{-\frac{d}{8}}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}
(here we use
\limsup\widetilde{\sigma}<\infty
to get the appropriate form of
\beta
from (
1.10
)). Now observe that the Fourier transform of
\varphi(x)
is given by

\widehat{\varphi}(k)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi}\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right),

(A.76)

where the inequality follows from
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2\pi}}
. Therefore
(.2)
holds.

For Assumption
B
, we first use Lemma
A.11
to see that
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=6^{-\frac{d}{2}}q_{\varphi}^{5}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
. Therefore
(.1)
can be seen to hold with
\rho=6^{-\frac{1}{2}}\liminf\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
. This also provides a lower bound on
h(d)
. After noting that
\log\beta(d)<0
, we have

\frac{\log h(d)}{\log\beta(d)}\leq\frac{-\frac{d}{2}\log 6+\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}{-\frac{d}{8}\log 2+\frac{1}{4}\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}\leq\frac{4\log 6-8\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}{\log 2-2\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}.

(A.77)

Note that
\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq 0
. By taking the derivative of the map
x\mapsto\frac{4\log 6-8x}{\log 2-2x}
for
x\leq 0
we can find that it is maximised at
x=0
. Therefore

\frac{\log h(d)}{\log\beta(d)}\leq 4\frac{\log 6}{\log 2}=4\log_{2}6.

(A.78)

Since this is finite, we have proven Assumption
(.2)
.
∎

A.4
Coordinate-Cauchy Calculations

Recall that for
\gamma>0
and
0<\mathcal{A}\leq\left(\gamma\pi\right)^{d}
, the Coordinate-Cauchy RCM is defined by having

\varphi(x)=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\left(\gamma\pi\right)^{d}}\prod^{d}_{j=1}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+x^{2}_{j}},

(A.79)

where
x=\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma A.13
.

For the Coordinate-Cauchy RCM,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{n\gamma\pi}\right)^{d},\qquad\forall n\geq 1,

(A.80)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\star n_{1}\star n_{2}\cdot n_{3}}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}}\left(\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}}{\left(n_{1}+n_{2}\right)\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)\left(n_{2}+n_{3}\right)\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\right)^{d},\qquad\forall n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 1.

(A.81)

In particular,

\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\mathcal{A}^{5}\left(\frac{5}{36\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\right)^{d},\qquad\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(\frac{1}{10\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\right)^{d},\qquad\varphi^{\star 2\star 2\cdot 2}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\mathcal{A}^{6}\left(\frac{3}{32\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\right)^{d}.

(A.82)

Proof.

We begin with the simplification that
q_{\varphi}=\mathcal{A}
is set to be equal to
1
(by a spatial scaling choice).

Like for the Hyper-Cubic model, the factorisable structure of the adjacency function means that we only need to evaluate the answers for the
1
-dimensional model, and then we can take the result to the power
d
to get the
d
-dimensional answer. Let the
1
-dimensional adjacency function be denoted

\varphi_{1}(x)=\frac{\gamma}{\pi\left(\gamma^{2}+x^{2}\right)}.

(A.83)

By well-known complex analysis techniques, the Fourier transform of this function is given by

\widehat{\varphi}_{1}(k)=\text{e}^{-\gamma\left\lvert k\right\rvert}

(A.84)

for
k\in\mathbb{R}
. Then by the Fourier inversion formula, for
n\geq 1
,

\varphi_{1}^{\star n}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n\gamma\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}k=\frac{1}{\gamma\pi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\text{e}^{-nk}\mathrm{d}k=\frac{1}{n\gamma\pi}.

(A.85)

The calculation is a little more complicated for the remaining objects. For
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 1
,

\varphi_{1}^{\star n_{1}\star n_{2}\cdot n_{3}}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\gamma\lvert k\rvert-n_{2}\gamma\lvert k-l\rvert-n_{3}\gamma\lvert l\rvert}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}l\\
=\frac{1}{\left(2\gamma\pi\right)^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\lvert k\rvert-n_{2}\lvert k-l\rvert-n_{3}\lvert l\rvert}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}l.

(A.86)

For
l\geq 0
, the
k
-integral can then be partitioned as

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\lvert k\rvert-n_{2}\lvert k-l\rvert}\mathrm{d}k

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{l}\text{e}^{-n_{1}k-n_{2}k+n_{2}l}\mathrm{d}k+\int^{l}_{0}\text{e}^{-n_{1}k+n_{2}k-n_{2}l}\mathrm{d}k+\int^{0}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{n_{1}k+n_{2}k-n_{2}l}\mathrm{d}k

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\left(\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}+\text{e}^{-n_{2}l}\right)+\begin{cases}l\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}&\colon n_{1}=n_{2}\\
\frac{1}{n_{1}-n_{2}}\left(\text{e}^{-n_{2}l}-\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}\right)&\colon n_{1}\neq n_{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+l\right)\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}&\colon n_{1}=n_{2}\\
\frac{2n_{1}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{2}l}-\frac{2n_{2}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}&\colon n_{1}\neq n_{2}.\end{cases}

(A.87)

The calculation is performed similarly for
l<0
, and we get

\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\lvert k\rvert-n_{2}\lvert k-l\rvert}\mathrm{d}k=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\left\lvert l\right\rvert\right)\text{e}^{-n_{1}\left\lvert l\right\rvert}&\colon n_{1}=n_{2}\\
\frac{2n_{1}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{2}\left\lvert l\right\rvert}-\frac{2n_{2}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\left\lvert l\right\rvert}&\colon n_{1}\neq n_{2}\end{cases}

(A.88)

for all
l\in\mathbb{R}
.

For
n_{1}=n_{2}
we then get

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\star n_{1}\star n_{1}\cdot n_{3}}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\left\lvert l\right\rvert\right)\text{e}^{-\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)\left\lvert l\right\rvert}\mathrm{d}l

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)}+\frac{1}{\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2n_{1}+n_{3}}{2n_{1}\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}.

(A.89)

Using
n_{1}=n_{2}=2
and
n_{3}=1
, and
n_{1}=n_{2}=2
and
n_{3}=2
gives us two of our desired results. We are only left with
\varphi^{\star 1\star 2\cdot 3}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
.

For
n_{1}\neq n_{2}
we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(2\gamma\pi\right)^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\text{e}^{-n_{1}\lvert k\rvert-n_{2}\lvert k-l\rvert-n_{3}\lvert l\rvert}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}l

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{0}\left(\frac{2n_{1}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{2}l}-\frac{2n_{2}}{n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}}\text{e}^{-n_{1}l}\right)\text{e}^{-n_{3}l}\mathrm{d}l

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{n_{1}}{\left(n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}\right)\left(n_{2}+n_{3}\right)}-\frac{n_{2}}{\left(n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}\right)\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}}{\left(n_{1}+n_{2}\right)\left(n_{1}+n_{3}\right)\left(n_{2}+n_{3}\right)}\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}\pi^{2}}.

(A.90)

Note that this expression reduces to the case (
A.89
) if
n_{1}=n_{2}
.
∎

Lemma A.14
.

The Coordinate-Cauchy RCM with
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
satisfies Assumptions
A
and
B
.

Proof.

For simplicity we scale space so that
q_{\varphi}=\mathcal{A}=1
. As argued analogously for the Gaussian RCM in Lemma
A.12
, the condition
\liminf\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
then becomes
\limsup\gamma<\infty
, and
\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)\leq 1
becomes
\gamma\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\pi}}
.

Since
\widehat{\varphi}(k)=\text{e}^{-\gamma\left\lVert k\right\rVert_{1}}\geq 0
, we know that
\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi^{\star m}(x)=\varphi^{\star m}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
for all
m\geq 1
. Therefore

\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi^{\star m}(x)=\left(m\gamma\pi\right)^{-d}.

(A.91)

Since
\gamma\pi\geq 1
, this approaches zero for all
m\geq 2
. Therefore
(.1)
holds with the choice
g(d)=\left(2\gamma\pi\right)^{-d}=2^{-d}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and
\beta(d)=2^{-\frac{d}{4}}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}
(here we use
\limsup\gamma<\infty
to get the appropriate form of
\beta
from (
1.10
)). Furthermore,
\gamma
cannot approach
0
and therefore our expression for
\widehat{\varphi}(k)
implies
(.2)
holds too.

From our prior calculations we have
\varphi^{\star 6}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=6^{-d}q_{\varphi}^{5}\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
and therefore
(.1)
can be seen to hold with
\rho=6^{-1}\liminf\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}>0
. It also allows us to lower bound
h(d)
. Noting that
\log\beta(d)<0
, this implies that

\frac{\log h(d)}{\log\beta(d)}\leq\frac{-d\log 6+\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}{-\frac{d}{4}\log 2+\frac{1}{4}\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)}\leq\frac{4\log 6-4\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}{\log 2-\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}}.

(A.92)

Note that
\log\varphi\left(\mathbf{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\leq 0
. By taking the derivative of the map
x\mapsto\frac{4\log 6-4x}{\log 2-x}
for
x\leq 0
we can find that it is maximised at
x=0
. Therefore

\frac{\log h(d)}{\log\beta(d)}\leq 4\frac{\log 6}{\log 2}=4\log_{2}6.

(A.93)

Since this is finite, we have proven Assumption
(.2)
.
∎
